## Problem Understanding

We need to find the minimum number of moves to remove all numbers from a sequence, where in each move we can remove a non-decreasing subsequence.

Key observations:
- A non-decreasing subsequence means we can pick elements where each element is greater than or equal to the previous one.
- We want to minimize the number of moves.

## Key Insight

This problem is equivalent to finding the minimum number of non-decreasing subsequences needed to cover the entire sequence.

This is actually related to the concept of Dilworth's theorem, which states that in any finite partially ordered set, the size of the largest antichain equals the minimum number of chains needed to partition the set.

In our case, we can think of this as: if we have a sequence, we want to find the minimum number of non-decreasing subsequences that cover all elements.

However, there's a simpler observation: we need to count how many "decreasing" transitions we have in the sequence.

## Algorithm

1. Find the length of the longest decreasing subsequence in the array.
2. Return that length.

To find the longest decreasing subsequence, we can use dynamic programming with O(n²) approach, but given the constraints (n ≤ 100000), we need an O(n log n) approach.

Actually, we need to find the longest decreasing subsequence, which is equivalent to finding the longest increasing subsequence in the negative array.

So:
- Create array B where B[i] = -A[i]
- Find the length of the longest increasing subsequence (LIS) of B
- This equals the length of the longest decreasing subsequence of A

We can use binary search based approach to find LIS in O(n log n).

## Implementation

```python
def longest_increasing_subsequence(arr):
    if not arr:
        return 0
    
    # Binary search approach to find LIS length
    tails = []  # tails[i] = smallest ending element of all increasing subsequences of length i+1
    
    for num in arr:
        # Binary search for the first element >= num
        left, right = 0, len(tails)
        while left < right:
            mid = (left + right) // 2
            if tails[mid] < num:
                left = mid + 1
            else:
                right = mid
        
        # If left == len(tails), we need to extend the sequence
        if left == len(tails):
            tails.append(num)
        else:
            tails[left] = num
    
    return len(tails)

# Read input
n = int(input())
arr = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Find the longest decreasing subsequence in original array
# This equals the length of LIS in the negative array
neg_arr = [-x for x in arr]
lis_length = longest_increasing_subsequence(neg_arr)

print(lis_length)
```

```python
def longest_increasing_subsequence(arr):
    if not arr:
        return 0
    
    # Binary search approach to find LIS length
    tails = []  # tails[i] = smallest ending element of all increasing subsequences of length i+1
    
    for num in arr:
        # Binary search for the first element >= num
        left, right = 0, len(tails)
        while left < right:
            mid = (left + right) // 2
            if tails[mid] < num:
                left = mid + 1
            else:
                right = mid
        
        # If left == len(tails), we need to extend the sequence
        if left == len(tails):
            tails.append(num)
        else:
            tails[left] = num
    
    return len(tails)

# Read input
n = int(input())
arr = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Find the length of the longest decreasing subsequence
# This equals the length of the LIS in array with elements negated
neg_arr = [-x for x in arr]
lis_length = longest_increasing_subsequence(neg_arr)

print(lis_length)
```

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the minimum number of moves to remove all numbers from a sequence, where in each move we can remove any non-decreasing subsequence.

Key insights:
- A non-decreasing subsequence means each element is greater than or equal to the previous one
- We want to minimize the number of such moves

## Analysis

Let me think about this differently. Instead of thinking about removing elements, let's think about what elements can be removed together in one move.

In one move, we can remove any non-decreasing subsequence. This means if we have elements in increasing order (or equal), we can remove them all together.

The key insight is that this problem is equivalent to finding the minimum number of non-decreasing subsequences that cover the entire sequence.

This is actually related to the concept of "Dilworth's theorem" in combinatorics, which states that in any finite partially ordered set, the size of the largest antichain equals the minimum number of chains needed to partition the set.

In our case:
- Elements can be grouped into non-decreasing subsequences
- If we have elements that are in decreasing order, they cannot be in the same non-decreasing subsequence
- The maximum number of decreasing pairs (or the length of the longest decreasing subsequence) gives us the minimum number of moves needed

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, this is equivalent to finding the length of the longest **decreasing** subsequence.

Why?
- If we have a decreasing subsequence of length k, then these k elements cannot be in the same non-decreasing subsequence (since in a non-decreasing sequence, each element must be ≥ the previous one)
- Each element in this decreasing subsequence must be in a different move
- Therefore, we need at least k moves

And it's also possible to achieve k moves - we can group the remaining elements in non-decreasing ways.

Actually, let me think again with examples:

Sample 1: [1, 2, 3]
- All elements are in non-decreasing order, so we can remove all in 1 move
- Longest decreasing subsequence has length 1

Sample 2: [4, 1, 2, 3]
- We can remove [1, 2, 3] in one move (they form a non-decreasing subsequence)
- We're left with [4], which can be removed in another move
- Total: 2 moves
- Longest decreasing subsequence: [4, 1] or [4, 2] or [4, 3] - length 2

Wait, actually there's a simpler way: we can find the length of the longest decreasing subsequence using a standard DP approach, but this might be O(n²).

Alternatively, I recall that in this type of problem (removing non-decreasing subsequences), the minimum number of moves equals the length of the longest decreasing subsequence.

So I'll implement an efficient solution to find the longest decreasing subsequence.

## Implementation

```python
def longest_decreasing_subsequence(arr):
    if not arr:
        return 0
    
    n = len(arr)
    # dp[i] = length of longest decreasing subsequence ending at index i
    dp = [1] * n
    
    for i in range(1, n):
        for j in range(i):
            if arr[j] > arr[i]:  # decreasing condition
                dp[i] = max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1)
    
    return max(dp)

# Read input
n = int(input())
arr = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Find the length of the longest decreasing subsequence
result = longest_decreasing_subsequence(arr)

print(result)
```

```python
import bisect

def longest_decreasing_subsequence(arr):
    if not arr:
        return 0
    
    # We want longest decreasing subsequence
    # We can transform: reverse the array and find longest increasing subsequence
    # Or just simulate decreasing
    
    tails = []
    
    for num in arr:
        # Find the first element in tails that is <= num (for decreasing)
        # Actually we want decreasing: we want to find position where we can extend
        # For decreasing, we want to find position where tails[i] > num (we can extend)
        
        # Find the rightmost position where tails[i] > num
        # This is equivalent to finding the leftmost position where tails[i] <= num
        pos = -1
        for i in range(len(tails)):
            if tails[i] > num:
                pos = i
                break
        
        if pos == -1:
            # We need to extend the sequence
            tails.append(num)
        else:
            # Replace tails[pos] with num
            tails[pos] = num
    
    return len(tails)

# But this is still O(n²)

# Better: use bisect to maintain tails for decreasing
def longest_decreasing_subsequence(arr):
    if not arr:
        return 0
    
    # For longest decreasing subsequence, we can transform to longest increasing
    # by taking negative values and reversing the array
    
    # Alternative: use the fact that longest decreasing is equivalent to 
    # longest increasing on negative values
    
    neg_arr = [-x for x in arr]
    
    # Now find longest increasing subsequence in neg_arr
    tails = []
    for num in neg_arr:
        # Find position where num should be inserted to keep sorted
        pos = bisect.bisect_left(tails, num)
        if pos == len(tails):
            tails.append(num)
        else:
            tails[pos] = num
    
    return len(tails)

# Read input
n = int(input())
arr = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Find length of longest decreasing subsequence
result = longest_decreasing_subsequence(arr)

print(result)
```

This solution finds the length of the longest decreasing subsequence in O(n log n) time, which is efficient enough for n = 100000.

```python
import bisect

n = int(input())
arr = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Find longest decreasing subsequence using negative transformation
# Longest decreasing subsequence in arr = longest increasing subsequence in [-x for x in arr]
neg_arr = [-x for x in arr]

tails = []
for num in neg_arr:
    pos = bisect.bisect_left(tails, num)
    if pos == len(tails):
        tails.append(num)
    else:
        tails[pos] = num

print(len(tails))
```

## Implementation

I'll use dynamic programming to find the longest decreasing subsequence:

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    
    # dp[i] = length of longest decreasing subsequence ending at index i
    dp = [1] * n
    
    for i in range(1, n):
        for j in range(i):
            if arr[j] > arr[i]:  # decreasing
                dp[i] = max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1)
    
    return max(dp)

print(solve())
```

Actually, I can compute the longest decreasing subsequence in O(n log n) using a different approach:

For longest decreasing subsequence, I can transform the problem to longest increasing subsequence by negating values, then use patience sorting.

Longest decreasing subsequence = Longest increasing subsequence of negated array.

So I can use the standard O(n log n) algorithm for LIS.

```python
def longest_increasing_subsequence(arr):
    if not arr:
        return 0
    
    # Use binary search to maintain the tails of increasing subsequences
    tails = []
    
    for x in arr:
        # Binary search for the first element >= x
        left, right = 0, len(tails)
        while left < right:
            mid = (left + right) // 2
            if tails[mid] < x:
                left = mid + 1
            else:
                right = mid
        
        if left == len(tails):
            tails.append(x)
        else:
            tails[left] = x
    
    return len(tails)

# For longest decreasing subsequence, negate the array
def longest_decreasing_subsequence(arr):
    return longest_increasing_subsequence([-x for x in arr])

n = int(input())
arr = list(map(int, input().split()))

result = longest_decreasing_subsequence(arr)
print(result)
```

- Elements: -4, -1, -2, -3
- Increasing subsequence: -4, -1 (length 2), or -4, -2 (length 2), etc.
- The longest increasing subsequence is length 2 ✓

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sequence of N numbers
2. In one move, we can choose a non-decreasing subsequence and remove it
3. We need to find the minimum number of moves to remove all numbers

## Key Insight

This problem is essentially about finding the minimum number of non-decreasing subsequences needed to cover the entire sequence.

This is equivalent to finding the length of the longest strictly decreasing subsequence. Here's why:

- If we have a strictly decreasing subsequence of length k, then we need at least k moves because each element in this decreasing sequence cannot be part of the same non-decreasing subsequence (since a non-decreasing sequence can't have decreasing elements).
- We can always achieve k moves by placing each element of the decreasing subsequence in its own non-decreasing subsequence.

## Algorithm

The minimum number of moves = length of the longest strictly decreasing subsequence.

To find the longest strictly decreasing subsequence, we can use the patience sorting algorithm or simply traverse the array.

Actually, let me think more carefully:

If we have a strictly decreasing subsequence, we can only pick non-decreasing subsequences. So any strictly decreasing pattern requires separate moves.

The key insight is that this is equivalent to finding the length of the longest strictly decreasing subsequence.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Find the longest strictly decreasing subsequence
2. The length of this subsequence gives us the minimum number of moves

- [1,2,3]: Longest strictly decreasing subsequence has length 1 → answer = 1
- [4,1,2,3]: Longest strictly decreasing subsequence is [4,1] (length 2) or [4,2] (length 2) → answer = 2

```python
    # For decreasing subsequences, we can use the fact that it's equivalent to the length of LIS in the reversed sequence
    
    # Actually, the minimum number of non-decreasing subsequences needed = length of longest strictly decreasing subsequence
    
    # We can find this by dynamic programming, but O(n^2) might be too slow

    # By Dilworth's theorem, minimum number of chains (non-decreasing subsequences) = size of largest antichain
    
    # In this case, antichain = longest strictly decreasing subsequence
    
    # We can compute longest decreasing subsequence with O(n^2) but need better
    
    # Actually, let me implement a simpler approach: the answer is the length of the longest strictly decreasing subsequence

    # Let's create a new array with elements in reverse order, and find LIS

    def lis_length(arr):
        if not arr:
            return 0
        
        # Use patience sorting with binary search
        tails = []
        
        for num in arr:
            # Find the leftmost position where num can be placed
            left, right = 0, len(tails)
            while left < right:
                mid = (left + right) // 2
                if tails[mid] < num:
                    left = mid + 1
                else:
                    right = mid
            
            # If left == len(tails), append
            if left == len(tails):
                tails.append(num)
            else:
                tails[left] = num
                
        return len(tails)
    
    # The length of LIS in reverse array gives us the length of longest decreasing subsequence
    # in original array
    result = lis_length(rev_arr)
    
    return result
```

```python
def longest_decreasing_subsequence_length(arr):
    if not arr:
        return 0
    
    n = len(arr)
    # dp[i] = length of longest decreasing subsequence ending at i
    dp = [1] * n
    
    for i in range(1, n):
        for j in range(i):
            if arr[j] > arr[i]:  # decreasing
                dp[i] = max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1)
    
    return max(dp) if dp else 0
```

- This is already non-decreasing, so we can remove it in 1 move
- Longest decreasing subsequence: length 1

**Example 2:** [4, 1, 2, 3]
- We need 2 moves

- Longest decreasing subsequence: [4, 1] or [4], length 2

Example 2: [4,1,2,3] - strictly decreasing subsequence [4,1] has length 2, so answer is 2 ✓

## Implementation Approach

I'll use dynamic programming to find the length of the longest strictly decreasing subsequence:

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    
    # dp[i] = length of longest strictly decreasing subsequence ending at index i
    dp = [1] * n
    
    for i in range(1, n):
        for j in range(i):
            if arr[j] > arr[i]:  # strictly decreasing
                dp[i] = max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1)
    
    # Answer is the maximum value in dp array
    return max(dp)
```

Wait, let me double-check the logic. Actually, I think I need to reconsider.

The minimum number of moves to remove all elements using non-decreasing subsequences equals the length of the longest strictly decreasing subsequence.

Alternative approach: 
The answer is equal to the length of the longest decreasing subsequence.

We can solve this using a greedy approach with binary search (similar to patience sorting).

```python
import bisect

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    
    # We want the length of longest strictly decreasing subsequence
    # We can use greedy approach: maintain a list of the smallest tail elements
    # of decreasing subsequences of various lengths
    
    tails = []  # tails[i] is the smallest ending element of a decreasing subsequence of length i+1
    
    for num in arr:
        # Find position where we can extend or replace
        # We want strictly decreasing
        pos = bisect.bisect_left(tails, num)  # find first element >= num
        
        if pos == len(tails):
            # num is larger than all elements in tails, so we extend
            tails.append(num)
        else:
            # replace the element at pos with num (making it smaller)
            tails[pos] = num
    
    return len(tails)

print(solve())
```

Wait, this is actually for longest increasing subsequence. For decreasing, we need to reverse the logic.

For strictly decreasing subsequence:
- We want to find the length of the longest strictly decreasing subsequence
- We can use the fact that this equals the length of the longest increasing subsequence of the negative array

Actually, let me do it directly:

For longest decreasing subsequence:
- We can maintain tails as the smallest tail of decreasing subsequences of various lengths
- Each step: we want to find where to place the current element in a way that maintains strictly decreasing order

```python
import bisect

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    
    # For longest strictly decreasing subsequence
    # We maintain tails where tails[i] is the smallest tail of a decreasing subsequence of length i+1
    # For decreasing, we want to place elements such that tails is in decreasing order
    
    tails = []  # will store elements in decreasing order
    
    for num in arr:
        # Find the first element in tails that is <= num (for strictly decreasing)
        # Since we want strictly decreasing, if tails is [a,b,c] with a>b>c, 
        # we want to find first element >= num to replace it
        
        pos = bisect.bisect_right(tails, num)  # first element > num
        
        if pos == len(tails):
            tails.append(num)
        else:
            tails[pos] = num
    
    # Actually, let me think again
    # For strictly decreasing: we want the subsequence to be strictly decreasing
    # We can transform the problem: find LIS of negative array
    
    # Or: find the longest decreasing subsequence by doing:
    # tails = [] # smallest tail elements of decreasing subsequences of different lengths
    # For each num, we want to find the rightmost position where num < tails[i]
    
    # Actually, the standard approach for longest decreasing subsequence:
    # Use bisect_right of negative values
    
    # Simpler: take the negative of the array and find LIS
    neg_arr = [-x for x in arr]
    
    # Now find LIS of neg_arr
    tails = []
    for num in neg_arr:
        pos = bisect.bisect_right(tails, num)
        if pos == len(tails):
            tails.append(num)
        else:
            tails[pos] = num
    
    return len(tails)

print(solve())
```

This should work. The longest decreasing subsequence of input array equals the longest increasing subsequence of the negative array.

Let me verify with examples:
- [1,2,3]: negative is [-1,-2,-3], LIS of this is 1 (e.g., [-1]) → answer 1 ✓
- [4,1,2,3]: negative is [-4,-1,-2,-3], LIS of this is 2 (e.g., [-4,-1] or [-4,-2] or [-4,-3]) → answer 2 ✓

Perfect!

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sequence of N numbers
2. In one move, we can remove a non-decreasing subsequence (not necessarily contiguous)
3. We want to find the minimum number of moves to remove all numbers

## Key Insight

This problem is asking for the minimum number of non-decreasing subsequences needed to cover the entire sequence.

This is equivalent to finding the length of the longest strictly decreasing subsequence. Here's why:

- If we have a strictly decreasing subsequence of length k, we need at least k moves to remove all elements
- We can always achieve this by removing one non-decreasing subsequence per element in the decreasing sequence
- This is a well-known result in combinatorics - the minimum number of non-decreasing subsequences needed equals the length of the longest strictly decreasing subsequence

## How it works

1. Find the longest strictly decreasing subsequence (LDS)
2. The answer is the length of this LDS

## Algorithm

1. Use dynamic programming to find the longest strictly decreasing subsequence
2. For each position, we track the length of the longest decreasing subsequence ending at that position
3. We can optimize this by using a different approach: for each element, we look at all previous elements that are greater than the current one

However, there's a more efficient approach: the minimum number of non-decreasing subsequences needed to cover a sequence equals the length of the longest strictly decreasing subsequence.

## Step-by-step solution

1. Read input
2. Find the longest strictly decreasing subsequence
3. Return its length

## Implementation

```python
def longest_strictly_decreasing_subsequence(arr):
    n = len(arr)
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    
    # dp[i] = length of longest strictly decreasing subsequence ending at index i
    dp = [1] * n
    
    for i in range(1, n):
        for j in range(i):
            if arr[j] > arr[i]:  # strictly decreasing
                dp[i] = max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1)
    
    return max(dp)

# Read input
n = int(input())
arr = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Find minimum moves = length of longest strictly decreasing subsequence
result = longest_strictly_decreasing_subsequence(arr)

print(result)
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a sequence of N numbers
2. In one move, we can remove any non-decreasing subsequence (not necessarily contiguous)
3. We need to find the minimum number of moves to remove all numbers

## Key Insight

This problem is essentially about finding the minimum number of non-decreasing subsequences needed to cover the entire array.

This is equivalent to finding the length of the longest **strictly decreasing** subsequence in the array. Here's why:

- If we have a strictly decreasing subsequence of length k, then we need at least k moves (since each move can remove at most one element from a non-decreasing subsequence)
- We can always achieve exactly k moves by creating k non-decreasing subsequences that each contain one element from each position in the decreasing subsequence

- If there's a strictly decreasing subsequence of length k, then we can't remove all elements in fewer than k moves
- We can construct k non-decreasing subsequences (one for each element in the decreasing subsequence) and each move removes one of these subsequences

## Solution

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    arr = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    
    # Find the length of the longest strictly decreasing subsequence
    # We can use a simple O(n^2) DP for now, but we can optimize
    
    # dp[i] = length of longest decreasing subsequence ending at index i
    dp = [1] * n
    
    for i in range(1, n):
        for j in range(i):
            if arr[j] > arr[i]:  # strictly decreasing
                dp[i] = max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1)
    
    # Answer is the maximum value in dp
    return max(dp)

print(solve())
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Find the number of k-divisible numbers on the segment [a, b]. In other words you need to find the number of such integer values x that a ≤ x ≤ b and x is divisible by k.


-----Input-----

The only line contains three space-separated integers k, a and b (1 ≤ k ≤ 10^18; - 10^18 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 10^18).


-----Output-----

Print the required number.


-----Examples-----
Input
1 1 10

Output
10

Input
2 -4 4

Output
5### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the count of integers x in the range [a, b] that are divisible by k.

For example:
- k=1, a=1, b=10: All numbers from 1 to 10 are divisible by 1, so answer is 10
- k=2, a=-4, b=4: Numbers divisible by 2 in [-4,4] are: -4, -2, 0, 2, 4 → total 5

## Key Insight

Instead of iterating through all numbers (which would be impossible with large ranges), we can use mathematics.

The numbers divisible by k form an arithmetic sequence: 0, k, 2k, 3k, ... or -k, 0, k, 2k, ...

We need to count how many such numbers fall within [a, b].

## Mathematical Approach

For a range [a, b], the count of multiples of k is:
**floor(b/k) - floor((a-1)/k)**

This works because:
- floor(b/k) gives the count of multiples of k that are ≤ b
- floor((a-1)/k) gives the count of multiples of k that are ≤ (a-1)
- Their difference gives us multiples of k in [a, b]

However, we need to be careful with negative numbers and edge cases.

## Alternative Approach (More Robust)

We can count multiples of k in [a,b] as:
**floor(b/k) - floor((a-1)/k)**

But we need to handle negative division correctly.

Actually, let me think of it differently:

The multiples of k in [a,b] are those where x = m*k for some integer m, and a ≤ m*k ≤ b.

So we need: a ≤ m*k ≤ b

For k > 0:
- m ≥ ceil(a/k)
- m ≤ floor(b/k)

So the count is: floor(b/k) - ceil(a/k) + 1

For k < 0: Since k ≥ 1, we don't need to worry about negative k.

Wait, the constraint says k ≥ 1, so k is always positive.

## Implementation Strategy

For positive k:
- Find the smallest multiple of k ≥ a: ceil(a/k)
- Find the largest multiple of k ≤ b: floor(b/k)
- Count = floor(b/k) - ceil(a/k) + 1

But we need to be careful with negative numbers in ceiling and floor functions.

In Python, we can use:
- floor division: // (for positive numbers)
- For negative numbers, // is floor division

For ceiling of a/k: ceil(a/k) = -floor(-a/k)

So:
- ceil(a/k) = -(-a // k) if a is negative, but this needs careful handling.

Actually, simpler approach:
- Count of multiples of k in [a,b] = (b // k) - ((a - 1) // k)

This works because:
- (b // k) counts multiples of k that are ≤ b
- ((a - 1) // k) counts multiples of k that are ≤ (a-1)
- The difference is multiples of k in [a,b]

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: k=1, a=1, b=10
- b//k = 10//1 = 10
- (a-1)//k = 0//1 = 0
- Result = 10 - 0 = 10 ✓

Example 2: k=2, a=-4, b=4
- b//k = 4//2 = 2
- (a-1)//k = (-5)//2 = -3 (in Python, -5//2 = -3)
- Result = 2 - (-3) = 5 ✓

Perfect!

## Final Solution

```python
k, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Count of multiples of k in [a, b]
# = floor(b/k) - floor((a-1)/k)
result = (b // k) - ((a - 1) // k)
print(result)
```

result = (b // k) - ((a - 1) // k)
print(result)

Find the number of k-divisible numbers on the segment [a, b]. In other words you need to find the number of such integer values x that a ≤ x ≤ b and x is divisible by k.


-----Input-----

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the count of integers x in the range [a, b] that are divisible by k.

For example:
- If k=2, a=-4, b=4: numbers divisible by 2 are -4, -2, 0, 2, 4 → count = 5

## Mathematical Approach

Instead of iterating through all numbers (which is impossible with large ranges), I'll use a mathematical formula.

The key insight is that the count of multiples of k in range [a, b] can be calculated as:

**Count = (number of multiples of k ≤ b) - (number of multiples of k < a)**

For any range [0, n], the number of multiples of k is ⌊n/k⌋.

But we need to be careful with negative numbers and the exact boundaries.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Count multiples of k from 1 to b**: This is ⌊b/k⌋
2. **Count multiples of k from 1 to a-1**: This is ⌊(a-1)/k⌋
3. **Subtract**: Answer = ⌊b/k⌋ - ⌊(a-1)/k⌋

However, this doesn't work directly for negative numbers because of how floor division works with negatives.

Let me think of a better approach:

The count of multiples of k in [a, b] = (number of multiples of k ≤ b) - (number of multiples of k ≤ a-1)

For any integer n, the count of multiples of k that are ≤ n is ⌊n/k⌋ when n ≥ 0, but we need to be careful with negative numbers.

Actually, ⌊n/k⌋ gives us exactly the count of multiples of k that are ≤ n for any integer n.

So the answer is: ⌊b/k⌋ - ⌊(a-1)/k⌋

Wait, let me verify with the example:
- k=2, a=-4, b=4
- ⌊4/2⌋ = 2
- ⌊(-5)/2⌋ = -3 (since -5/2 = -2.5, floor is -3)
- Answer = 2 - (-3) = 5 ✓

This works!

## Edge Cases

- When a = b: should return 1 if a % k == 0, else 0
- The formula ⌊b/k⌋ - ⌊(a-1)/k⌋ should work for all cases including negative numbers

## Implementation

```python
k, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Count of multiples of k in range [a, b]
# = (number of multiples of k <= b) - (number of multiples of k <= a-1)
# = floor(b/k) - floor((a-1)/k)

count_b = b // k
count_a_minus_1 = (a - 1) // k

result = count_b - count_a_minus_1
print(result)
```

Wait, I need to be careful about the floor division with negative numbers in Python. In Python, `//` is floor division, which works exactly as we need it for negative numbers.

Let me verify with the first example:
- k=1, a=1, b=10
- count_b = 10 // 1 = 10
- count_a_minus_1 = (1-1) // 1 = 0 // 1 = 0
- result = 10 - 0 = 10 ✓

Second example:
- k=2, a=-4, b=4
- count_b = 4 // 2 = 2
- count_a_minus_1 = (-4-1) // 2 = -5 // 2 = -3 (since -5/2 = -2.5, floor is -3)
- result = 2 - (-3) = 5 ✓

Perfect! The solution works.

```python
k, a, b = map(int, input().split())

count_b = b // k
count_a_minus_1 = (a - 1) // k

result = count_b - count_a_minus_1
print(result)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the count of integers x in the range [a, b] that are divisible by k.

## Key Insight

Instead of iterating through all numbers (which would be too slow for large ranges), we can use mathematics.

The number of multiples of k in a range [a, b] can be calculated as:
- Count of multiples of k from 1 to b: ⌊b/k⌋
- Count of multiples of k from 1 to a-1: ⌊(a-1)/k⌋
- Answer = ⌊b/k⌋ - ⌊(a-1)/k⌋

However, we need to be careful about negative numbers and the floor function.

## Alternative Approach

The count of multiples of k in [a, b] is:
- Count of multiples of k from 1 to b: ⌊b/k⌋ (when b ≥ 0)
- But when a ≤ 0, we need to handle negative multiples carefully.

Let me think about this differently:

The multiples of k in [a, b] are: k×m, where m is an integer such that a ≤ k×m ≤ b.

So we need: a/k ≤ m ≤ b/k

The number of integer values m in this range is: ⌊b/k⌋ - ⌊(a-1)/k⌋

Wait, let me verify this with the examples:

Example 1: k=1, a=1, b=10
- ⌊10/1⌋ - ⌊(1-1)/1⌋ = 10 - 0 = 10 ✓

Example 2: k=2, a=-4, b=4
- ⌊4/2⌋ - ⌊(-4-1)/2⌋ = ⌊2⌋ - ⌊(-5)/2⌋ = 2 - ⌊-2.5⌋ = 2 - (-3) = 5 ✓

Yes, this works! The floor function for negative numbers behaves correctly.

## Formula

Answer = ⌊b/k⌋ - ⌊(a-1)/k⌋

But we need to be careful about integer division and negative numbers in Python.

In Python, `//` is floor division, which works correctly for negative numbers.

## Edge Cases

- If a = b, we need to check if a is divisible by k
- The formula handles this naturally

## Implementation

```python
k, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Count of multiples of k in [a, b]
# = number of multiples of k from 1 to b - number of multiples of k from 1 to (a-1)
# = floor(b/k) - floor((a-1)/k)

count_b = b // k
count_a_minus = (a - 1) // k

result = count_b - count_a_minus
print(result)
```

Wait, let me double-check the logic with example 2:
- k=2, a=-4, b=4
- floor(4/2) = 2
- floor((-4-1)/2) = floor(-5/2) = floor(-2.5) = -3
- result = 2 - (-3) = 5 ✓

Perfect! This matches.

The formula works because:
- ⌊b/k⌋ gives the count of multiples of k from 0 to b (inclusive of multiples at endpoints)
- ⌊(a-1)/k⌋ gives the count of multiples of k from 0 to (a-1) (inclusive)
- The difference gives us the count from a to b

```python
k, a, b = map(int, input().split())

count_b = b // k
count_a_minus = (a - 1) // k

result = count_b - count_a_minus
print(result)
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the count of integers x in the range [a, b] that are divisible by k.

For example:
- If k=1, a=1, b=10: all numbers from 1 to 10 are divisible by 1, so answer is 10
- If k=2, a=-4, b=4: numbers divisible by 2 in [-4,4] are -4, -2, 0, 2, 4, so answer is 5

## Mathematical Approach

The key insight is that we can use the concept of "count of multiples of k in a range".

For a range [0, n], the number of multiples of k is: `n // k` (integer division).

For a general range [a, b], we can use:
- Count of multiples of k in [0, b] minus count of multiples of k in [0, a-1]

But we need to be careful with negative numbers and the fact that we're dealing with integer division.

## Step-by-step solution

1. **Find count of multiples of k in [0, b]**
   - If b ≥ 0: this is `b // k`
   - If b < 0: this is `(-b) // k` (but need to think about negative division)

2. **Find count of multiples of k in [0, a-1]**
   - If a-1 ≥ 0: this is `(a-1) // k`
   - If a-1 < 0: this is `(- (a-1)) // k`

Actually, there's a simpler and more robust approach:

The number of multiples of k in [a, b] = (number of multiples of k ≤ b) - (number of multiples of k < a)

For any integer n, the number of multiples of k that are ≤ n is:
- If n ≥ 0: `n // k`
- If n < 0: `-(n // k)` (but this isn't quite right for negative division)

Wait, let me reconsider the negative case.

For negative numbers, when we divide, Python's floor division for negative numbers rounds down.

Actually, the formula for count of multiples of k that are ≤ n is:
- `(n // k) + 1` if n ≥ 0 and n % k == 0
- `n // k` if n < 0

No, that's not right either.

Let me think differently. The multiples of k are: ..., -2k, -k, 0, k, 2k, ...

For any integer n, the largest multiple of k that is ≤ n is: `k * (n // k)` if n ≥ 0, but for negative n, floor division behaves differently.

Actually, the count of multiples of k in range [a, b] can be calculated as:
- Count of multiples of k ≤ b minus count of multiples of k < a

And the count of multiples of k ≤ n is:
- `(n // k) + 1` if n ≥ 0
- `(n // k)` if n < 0

Wait, let me verify with an example:
- Range [-4, 4], k = 2
- Multiples of 2 ≤ 4: 0, 2, 4 → count = 3
- Multiples of 2 < -4: -6, -8, ... but -6 < -4, -2 > -4, so multiples < -4 are -6, -8, ... 
- Actually, multiples < -4: -6, -8, -10, ... 

Better approach: the number of multiples of k that are ≤ n is:
- `(n // k) + 1` if n ≥ 0 and n % k == 0, but this is messy.

Actually, I recall a standard formula: the number of integers in [a,b] divisible by k is:
`((b // k) - ((a - 1) // k))`

Let me verify:
- For k=2, a=-4, b=4:
  - b//k = 4//2 = 2
  - (a-1) = -5, (-5)//2 = -3 (in Python, -5//2 = -3)
  - Answer = 2 - (-3) = 5 ✓

Another example:
- k=1, a=1, b=10:
  - b//k = 10//1 = 10
  - (a-1)//k = 0//1 = 0
  - Answer = 10 - 0 = 10 ✓

This seems to work!

## Why does `(b // k) - ((a - 1) // k)` work?

- `b // k` gives the count of multiples of k that are ≤ b
- `(a - 1) // k` gives the count of multiples of k that are ≤ (a-1)
- So `b // k - (a - 1) // k` gives the count of multiples of k that are in [a, b]

This formula works because:
- If there's a multiple of k exactly at a, it will be counted
- The range [a, b] is exactly what we want

## Implementation

```python
k, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Count of multiples of k in range [a, b]
# = count of multiples of k <= b - count of multiples of k < a
# = (b // k) - ((a - 1) // k)

result = (b // k) - ((a - 1) // k)
print(result)
```

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: k=1, a=1, b=10
- b//k = 10//1 = 10
- (a-1)//k = 0//1 = 0
- result = 10 - 0 = 10 ✓

Example 2: k=2, a=-4, b=4
- b//k = 4//2 = 2
- (a-1)//k = (-5)//2 = -3 (in Python, -5//2 = -3)
- result = 2 - (-3) = 5 ✓

Perfect!

```python
k, a, b = map(int, input().split())
result = (b // k) - ((a - 1) // k)
print(result)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the count of integers x in the range [a, b] that are divisible by k.

For example:
- If k=1, a=1, b=10: all numbers from 1 to 10 are divisible by 1, so answer is 10
- If k=2, a=-4, b=4: numbers divisible by 2 are -4, -2, 0, 2, 4, so answer is 5

## Mathematical Approach

Instead of iterating through all numbers (which would be impossible for large ranges), we can use a mathematical formula.

The key insight is that we need to count how many multiples of k exist in the range [a, b].

For a range [0, n], the count of multiples of k is: `n // k` (integer division).

But our range is [a, b] where a can be negative.

We can use the idea: 
**Count of multiples of k in [a, b] = Count of multiples of k in [0, b] - Count of multiples of k in [0, a-1]**

However, when a is negative, we need to be careful about the boundaries.

A simpler and more direct approach:
- Count of multiples of k in [a, b] = `floor(b/k) - floor((a-1)/k)`

But we need to handle negative numbers correctly with floor division.

Actually, let me think more systematically:

The multiples of k in [a, b] are: k × m where m is an integer and a ≤ k × m ≤ b.

So: a ≤ k × m ≤ b
Then: a/k ≤ m ≤ b/k

The count of integers m in this range is: `floor(b/k) - floor((a-1)/k)` 

Wait, let me reconsider the standard approach:

For counting multiples of k in [l, r]:
- Count = floor(r/k) - floor((l-1)/k)

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: k=1, a=1, b=10
- floor(10/1) - floor((1-1)/1) = 10 - floor(0/1) = 10 - 0 = 10 ✓

Example 2: k=2, a=-4, b=4
- floor(4/2) - floor((-4-1)/2) = floor(2) - floor(-5/2) = 2 - floor(-2.5) = 2 - (-3) = 5 ✓

Yes, this works! The key is that for negative division, floor division in Python behaves correctly.

## Implementation

```python
k, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Count of multiples of k in [a, b]
# = floor(b/k) - floor((a-1)/k)
count = b // k - (a - 1) // k

print(count)
```

Wait, let me double-check the formula. The standard formula for counting multiples of k in [l, r] is:
`floor(r/k) - floor((l-1)/k)`

In Python, `//` is floor division, which works correctly for negative numbers.

Let me verify with example 2:
- k=2, a=-4, b=4
- b//k = 4//2 = 2
- (a-1)//k = (-4-1)//2 = (-5)//2 = -3 (since floor(-2.5) = -3)
- Result = 2 - (-3) = 5 ✓

Perfect!

```python
k, a, b = map(int, input().split())
count = b // k - (a - 1) // k
print(count)
```

But we need to handle the case when a ≤ 0, because floor division in Python for negative numbers behaves differently than mathematical floor.

This works because:
- ⌊b/k⌋ = count of multiples of k that are ≤ b
- ⌊(a-1)/k⌋ = count of multiples of k that are ≤ (a-1)
- Their difference gives multiples of k in range [a,b]

## Edge Cases

- When a = b: just check if a is divisible by k
- When a < 0: floor division will work correctly

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read k, a, b
2. Calculate count of multiples of k ≤ b: floor(b/k)
3. Calculate count of multiples of k ≤ (a-1): floor((a-1)/k)
4. Answer = count_b - count_a_minus_1

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: k=1, a=1, b=10
- ⌊10/1⌋ = 10
- ⌊(1-1)/1⌋ = ⌊0/1⌋ = 0
- Answer = 10 - 0 = 10 ✓

Example 2: k=2, a=-4, b=4
- ⌊4/2⌋ = 2
- ⌊(-4-1)/2⌋ = ⌊-5/2⌋ = -3 (since floor(-2.5) = -3)
- Answer = 2 - (-3) = 5 ✓

Wait, this gives 5, which matches. Let me verify:
Multiples of 2 in [-4,4]: -4, -2, 0, 2, 4 = 5 numbers ✓

But actually: ⌊(a-1)/k⌋ = ⌊-5/2⌋ = -3, so 2 - (-3) = 5 ✓

The formula works!

```python
k, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Count of multiples of k in [a, b]
# = count of multiples of k <= b - count of multiples of k <= (a-1)
count_up_to_b = b // k
count_up_to_a_minus_1 = (a - 1) // k

result = count_up_to_b - count_up_to_a_minus_1
print(result)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the count of integers x in the range [a, b] that are divisible by k.

For example:
- If k=1, a=1, b=10: all numbers from 1 to 10 are divisible by 1, so answer is 10
- If k=2, a=-4, b=4: numbers divisible by 2 are -4, -2, 0, 2, 4, so answer is 5

## Mathematical Approach

Instead of iterating through all numbers (which would be impossible for large ranges), we need a mathematical solution.

The key insight is that numbers divisible by k form an arithmetic sequence:
- k, 2k, 3k, 4k, ... (positive)
- 0, -k, -2k, -3k, ... (negative, including 0)

The count of multiples of k in range [a, b] can be calculated as:
- Count of multiples of k in [0, b] minus count of multiples of k in [0, a-1]

But we need to be careful with negative numbers and boundaries.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Count multiples of k from 0 to b**: This is `floor(b/k)` if b ≥ 0, or `floor(b/k)` (which can be negative) if b < 0.

2. **Count multiples of k from 0 to a-1**: This is `floor((a-1)/k)` if a-1 ≥ 0, or `floor((a-1)/k)` otherwise.

3. **Final answer**: Count from 0 to b minus count from 0 to a-1.

However, we need to handle negative numbers correctly. The general formula is:

**Answer = floor(b/k) - floor((a-1)/k)**

But we need to be careful about the floor function with negative numbers.

Actually, there's a simpler approach:
- The number of multiples of k in range [a, b] is:
  `floor(b/k) - floor((a-1)/k)`

Let me verify with the examples:

**Example 1**: k=1, a=1, b=10
- floor(10/1) = 10
- floor((1-1)/1) = floor(0/1) = 0
- Answer: 10 - 0 = 10 ✓

**Example 2**: k=2, a=-4, b=4
- floor(4/2) = 2
- floor((-4-1)/2) = floor(-5/2) = floor(-2.5) = -3
- Answer: 2 - (-3) = 5 ✓

Wait, this gives 2 - (-3) = 5, which is correct.

But let me verify the multiples: -4, -2, 0, 2, 4 → that's 5 numbers.

The formula works! 

## Implementation Considerations

Python's `//` operator for integers performs floor division, which is what we need for negative numbers.

Special case: when k is positive (which it always is, 1 ≤ k ≤ 10^18).

## Final Code

```python
k, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Count multiples of k in [a, b]
# = count of multiples in [0, b] - count of multiples in [0, a-1]
# = floor(b/k) - floor((a-1)/k)

count_b = b // k
count_a_minus_1 = (a - 1) // k

result = count_b - count_a_minus_1
print(result)
```

Wait, I need to be extra careful. Let me verify the logic with different cases.

Actually, the formula `floor(b/k) - floor((a-1)/k)` should work correctly because:
- For any integer x, the number of multiples of k in range [0, x] is floor(x/k)
- The number of multiples of k in range [a, b] is the number in [0, b] minus the number in [0, a-1]

This handles negative numbers correctly due to how floor division works in Python.

The code is ready:

```python
k, a, b = map(int, input().split())

count_b = b // k
count_a_minus_1 = (a - 1) // k

result = count_b - count_a_minus_1
```

Alice has just learned addition. However, she hasn't learned the concept of "carrying" fully — instead of carrying to the next column, she carries to the column two columns to the left.

For example, the regular way to evaluate the sum $2039 + 2976$ would be as shown:

However, Alice evaluates it as shown:

In particular, this is what she does:

add $9$ and $6$ to make $15$, and carry the $1$ to the column two columns to the left, i. e. to the column "$0$ $9$";

add $3$ and $7$ to make $10$ and carry the $1$ to the column two columns to the left, i. e. to the column "$2$ $2$";

add $1$, $0$, and $9$ to make $10$ and carry the $1$ to the column two columns to the left, i. e. to the column above the plus sign;

add $1$, $2$ and $2$ to make $5$;

add $1$ to make $1$.

Thus, she ends up with the incorrect result of $15005$.

Alice comes up to Bob and says that she has added two numbers to get a result of $n$. However, Bob knows that Alice adds in her own way. Help Bob find the number of ordered pairs of positive integers such that when Alice adds them, she will get a result of $n$. Note that pairs $(a, b)$ and $(b, a)$ are considered different if $a \ne b$.

The only line of each test case contains an integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 10^9$) — the number Alice shows Bob.


-----Output-----

For each test case, output one integer — the number of ordered pairs of positive integers such that when Alice adds them, she will get a result of $n$.

When adding digits at position i:
- If digit sum ≥ 10, she carries the carry value to position i-2 (two columns to the left)
- The digit at position i is taken as (digit sum) % 10

## Key Insight

In Alice's addition, the carry propagates to the left by 2 positions. This means the digits in the result are connected in a specific pattern.

Let me think about this differently. If we have two numbers a and b, when Alice adds them, the sum at each digit position depends on:
1. The digits at that position

3. Any carry to two positions to the left

- The digit at position i depends on digits at position i and position i-2 (with carry)
- The carry from position i goes to position i-2

Since carries go to the left by 2 positions, the carry at position i depends only on the digits at positions i+2, i+4, etc.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Alice has a special addition rule: when adding digits, she carries the result to the column **two columns to the left** instead of one.
2. We need to find the number of ordered pairs (a, b) of positive integers such that when Alice adds a and b, she gets result n.
3. Pairs (a,b) and (b,a) are different if a ≠ b.

## Key Insight

Let me analyze what happens when Alice adds two numbers with this special carry rule.

For any digit positions, when Alice adds two digits d1 and d2 from the same position, she gets:
- Sum = d1 + d2 + carry_in
- If sum ≥ 10, she carries 1 to the position two digits to the left
- The digit at current position becomes (sum % 10)

## Pattern Recognition

Let me think about how the carry propagates:
- When we have a carry of 1 from position i, it affects position i-2
- This means we have a special relationship between digits at positions that are two apart

## Mathematical Analysis

For Alice's addition to produce result n, we need to work backwards from the digits of n.

Let's consider the digits of n from right to left (least significant to most significant). For each digit position, we need to determine the possible digit pairs (a_digit, b_digit) that could produce that digit in Alice's sum.

However, there's a key insight: Alice's carry rule creates a dependency pattern where digits in positions that are two apart are connected through carry propagation.

## Key Observation

After analyzing the carry pattern, I realize that the constraint is simpler than it first appears. Let me think about this differently.

For any digit position i in the result:
- The digit at position i comes from the sum of corresponding digits in a and b, plus any carry from position i-2
- The carry from position i goes to position i-2

This creates a recursive relationship. However, I notice from the examples:

For n = 100: answer is 9
For n = 12: answer is 4
For n = 8: answer is 7

Looking at the pattern, I suspect that the answer might be related to the digits of n in a specific way.

## Deeper Analysis

Let me consider the structure. The carry goes to the position two columns left, not one. This means we can think of the number as composed of alternating "groups" of digits.

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Alice has just learned addition. However, she hasn't learned the concept of "carrying" fully — instead of carrying to the next column, she carries to the column two columns to the left.

For example, the regular way to evaluate the sum $2039 + 2976$ would be as shown:

However, Alice evaluates it as shown:

In particular, this is what she does:

add $9$ and $6$ to make $15$, and carry the $1$ to the column two columns to the left, i. e. to the column "$0$ $9$";

add $3$ and $7$ to make $10$ and carry the $1$ to the column two columns to the left, i. e. to the column "$2$ $2$";

add $1$, $0$, and $9$ to make $10$ and carry the $1$ to the column two columns to the left, i. e. to the column above the plus sign;

add $1$, $2$ and $2$ to make $5$;

add $1$ to make $1$.

Thus, she ends up with the incorrect result of $15005$.

Alice comes up to Bob and says that she has added two numbers to get a result of $n$. However, Bob knows that Alice adds in her own way. Help Bob find the number of ordered pairs of positive integers such that when Alice adds them, she will get a result of $n$. Note that pairs $(a, b)$ and $(b, a)$ are considered different if $a \ne b$.


-----Input-----

The input consists of multiple test cases. The first line contains an integer $t$ ($1 \leq t \leq 1000$) — the number of test cases. The description of the test cases follows.

The only line of each test case contains an integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 10^9$) — the number Alice shows Bob.


-----Output-----

For each test case, output one integer — the number of ordered pairs of positive integers such that when Alice adds them, she will get a result of $n$.


-----Examples-----

5
100
12
8
2021
10000
Output

99

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Alice has just learned addition. However, she hasn't learned the concept of "carrying" fully — instead of carrying to the next column, she carries to the column two columns to the left.

For example, the regular way to evaluate the sum $2039 + 2976$ would be as shown:

However, Alice evaluates it as shown:

In particular, this is what she does:

add $9$ and $6$ to make $15$, and carry the $1$ to the column two columns to the left, i. e. to the column "$0$ $9$";

add $3$ and $7$ to make $10$ and carry the $1$ to the column two columns to the left, i. e. to the column "$2$ $2$";

add $1$, $0$, and $9$ to make $10$ and carry the $1$ to the column two columns to the left, i. e. to the column above the plus sign;

add $1$, $2$ and $2$ to make $5$;

add $1$ to make $1$.

Thus, she ends up with the incorrect result of $15005$.

Alice comes up to Bob and says that she has added two numbers to get a result of $n$. However, Bob knows that Alice adds in her own way. Help Bob find the number of ordered pairs of positive integers such that when Alice adds them, she will get a result of $n$. Note that pairs $(a, b)$ and $(b, a)$ are considered different if $a \ne b$.


-----Input-----

The input consists of multiple test cases. The first line contains an integer $t$ ($1 \leq t \leq 1000$) — the number of test cases. The description of the test cases follows.

The only line of each test case contains an integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 10^9$) — the number Alice shows Bob.


-----Output-----

For each test case, output one integer — the number of ordered pairs of positive integers such that when Alice adds them, she will get a result of $n$.


-----Examples-----

Input
5
100
12
8
2021
10000
Output
9
4
7
44
99


-----Note-----

In the first test case, when Alice evaluates any of the sums $1 + 9$, $2 + 8$, $3 + 7$, $4 + 6$, $5 + 5$, $6 + 4$, $7 + 3$, $8 + 2$, or $9 + 1$, she will get a result of $100$. Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Alice has just learned addition. However, she hasn't learned the concept of "carrying" fully — instead of carrying to the next column, she carries to the column two columns to the left.

For example, the regular way to evaluate the sum $2039 + 2976$ would be as shown:

However, Alice evaluates it as shown:

In particular, this is what she does:

add $9$ and $6$ to make $15$, and carry the $1$ to the column two columns to the left, i. e. to the column "$0$ $9$";

add $3$ and $7$ to make $10$ and carry the $1$ to the column two columns to the left, i. e. to the column "$2$ $2$";

add $1$, $0$, and $9$ to make $10$ and carry the $1$ to the column two columns to the left, i. e. to the column above the plus sign;

add $1$, $2$ and $2$ to make $5$;

add $1$ to make $1$.

Thus, she ends up with the incorrect result of $15005$.

Alice comes up to Bob and says that she has added two numbers to get a result of $n$. However, Bob knows that Alice adds in her own way. Help Bob find the number of ordered pairs of positive integers such that when Alice adds them, she will get a result of $n$. Note that pairs $(a, b)$ and $(b, a)$ are considered different if $a \ne b$.


-----Input-----

The input consists of multiple test cases. The first line contains an integer $t$ ($1 \leq t \leq 1000$) — the number of test cases. The description of the test cases follows.

The only line of each test case contains an integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 10^9$) — the number Alice shows Bob.


-----Output-----

For each test case, output one integer — the number of ordered pairs of positive integers such that when Alice adds them, she will get a result of $n$.


-----Examples-----

Input
5
100
12
8
2021
10000
Output
9
4
7
44
99


-----Note-----

In the first test case, when Alice evaluates any of the sums $1 + 9$, $2 + 8$, $3 + 7$, $4 + 6$, $5 + 5$, $6 + 4$, $7 + 3$, $8 + 2$, or $9 + 1$, she will get a result of $100$. Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given an array $a$ of length $n$, and an integer $x$. You can perform the following operation as many times as you would like (possibly zero): replace two adjacent elements of the array by their sum. For example, if the initial array was $[3, 6, 9]$, in a single operation one can replace the last two elements by their sum, yielding an array $[3, 15]$, or replace the first two elements to get an array $[9, 9]$. Note that the size of the array decreases after each operation.

The beauty of an array $b=[b_1, \ldots, b_k]$ is defined as $\sum_{i=1}^k \left\lceil \frac{b_i}{x} \right\rceil$, which means that we divide each element by $x$, round it up to the nearest integer, and sum up the resulting values. For example, if $x = 3$, and the array is $[4, 11, 6]$, the beauty of the array is equal to $\left\lceil \frac{4}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{11}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{6}{3} \right\rceil = 2 + 4 + 2 = 8$.

Please determine the minimum and the maximum beauty you can get by performing some operations on the original array.


-----Input-----

The first input line contains a single integer $t$ — the number of test cases ($1 \le t \le 1000$).

The first line of each test case contains two integers $n$ and $x$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$, $1 \leq x \leq 10^9$).

The next line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \leq a_i \leq 10^9$), the elements of the array $a$.

It is guaranteed that the sum of values of $n$ over all test cases does not exceed $10^5$.


-----Output-----

For each test case output two integers — the minimal and the maximal possible beauty.


-----Examples-----

Input
2
3 3
3 6 9
3 3
6 4 11
Output
6 6
7 8


-----Note-----

In the first test case the beauty of the array does not change if we perform any operations.

In the second example we can leave the array unchanged to attain the maximum beauty, and to get the minimum beauty one can replace two elements $4$ and $11$ with their sum, yielding an array $[6, 15]$, which has its beauty equal to $7$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length `n` and an integer `x`
2. We can replace two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size by 1 each time)
3. Beauty is defined as the sum of ceiling of each element divided by x
4. We need to find the minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to keep elements as separate as possible
- The ceiling function ⌈a/x⌉ = (a + x - 1) // x
- Since ⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ ≥ ⌈(a+b)/x⌉ (this is a known inequality), the maximum beauty is achieved when we don't merge any elements
- So maximum beauty = sum of ⌈ai/x⌉ for all i

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to merge elements strategically
- When we merge two elements a and b, we replace ⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ with ⌈(a+b)/x⌉
- Since ⌈(a+b)/x⌉ ≤ ⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉, merging can only decrease or keep the beauty constant
- However, we need to find the minimum possible value

The key insight for minimum beauty is that we can think of this in terms of "excess" values when dividing by x.

When we divide any number by x, we get an integer part and a remainder (0 to x-1).
- If remainder is 0, ⌈a/x⌉ = a//x
- If remainder > 0, ⌈a/x⌉ = a//x + 1

The "excess" contribution is 1 for each number with positive remainder.

When we merge two numbers, we're essentially combining their remainders:
- If we have remainders r1 and r2, their sum is r1+r2
- ⌈(a+b)/x⌉ = (a+b)//x + 1 if (a+b)%x > 0, otherwise (a+b)//x
- The excess contribution becomes 1 if (r1+r2) >= x, otherwise 0

So the minimum beauty is (total_sum // x) + (number of excess remainders after optimal merging)

Actually, let's think differently:
- Total sum S = sum of all elements
- Base contribution from total sum: S // x
- Additional contribution from remainders: each positive remainder contributes 1 to the ceiling value
- When we merge elements with remainders, if their combined remainder ≥ x, we can eliminate one excess contribution

The minimum excess we can achieve is the number of "excess" groups we can eliminate.

Wait, let me reconsider: when we merge, we're combining remainders. The total remainder sum is fixed (sum of all remainders), and we want to minimize the number of "positive remainder" contributions.

Actually, the minimum beauty is: (total_sum // x) + max(0, (total_remainder + x - 1) // x - (total_remainder // x))

No, simpler: the minimum beauty is (sum of all elements) // x, plus some adjustment for how we can eliminate excess 1's through merging.

The excess we can eliminate depends on whether we can combine remainders to make them ≥ x.

The key insight: the minimum beauty equals (sum of all elements) // x + (number of "full" remainder units that can't be cancelled out).

Actually, let me think of it as:
- Each element contributes at least (value // x) to the beauty
- The excess (value % x) contributes either 0 or 1 depending on whether it's positive

When we merge two elements with remainders r1 and r2:
- If r1 + r2 >= x, then after merging, we get one contribution of 1 (since ceiling increases)
- If r1 + r2 < x, then after merging, we get 0 (no additional contribution)

The total "excess" (how many numbers have remainder > 0) can be minimized by merging.

The minimum number of excess contributions is the minimum number of "positive remainders" we can end up with after all possible merges.

The total sum of remainders is fixed. We can group them to see how many groups of size ≥ x we can form.

Actually, the minimum beauty is: (sum of all elements) // x + max(0, (sum of all remainders) - x * floor((sum of all remainders) / x))

Wait, that's just (sum of all elements) // x + (sum of all remainders) // x.

Let me check with the examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Sum = 18, sum//3 = 6
- Remainders: 0,0,0, sum = 0
- Minimum beauty = 6 + 0 = 6
- Maximum beauty = ⌈3/3⌉ + ⌈6/3⌉ + ⌈9/3⌉ = 1+2+3 = 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3  
- Sum = 21, sum//3 = 7
- Remainders: 0, 1, 2, sum = 3
- Sum of remainders = 3, (sum of remainders)//3 = 1
- Minimum beauty = 7 + 1 = 8? But expected is 7.

Wait, the expected minimum is 7. Let me check the example again.

They say: replace 4 and 11 with 15, giving [6,15]
- ⌈6/3⌉ = 2, ⌈15/3⌉ = 5, sum = 7

In terms of remainders:
- Original: 6%3=0, 4%3=1, 11%3=2 → total remainder sum = 3
- After merge: 6%3=0, 15%3=0 → total remainder sum = 0
- Base sum // x = 21//3 = 7
- Excess = 0 (since after merging, no elements have positive remainders)

So minimum beauty = (sum // x) + (some function of remainders)

Actually, the minimum beauty is simply the ceiling of the total sum divided by x?

⌈sum/x⌉ = ⌈21/3⌉ = 7 ✓

Is this always true?

Yes! Because if we merge all elements into one, we get an array with one element = sum, and beauty = ⌈sum/x⌉.

Can we get anything smaller? No, because each element contributes at least its integer division by x, and the sum of these minimums is at least sum//x, and since we have a positive remainder in some case, we can't go below ⌈sum/x⌉.

Actually, the sum of ⌈a_i/x⌉ ≥ ⌈sum(a_i)/x⌉ by the property of ceiling functions.

So minimum beauty = ⌈total_sum / x⌉

Wait, no: ⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ ≥ ⌈(a+b)/x⌉, so sum of ceilings ≥ ⌈sum/x⌉.

And if we merge all elements, we get exactly ⌈sum/x⌉.

Therefore, the minimum beauty is ⌈total_sum / x⌉.

For maximum beauty: we don't merge any elements, so it's sum of ⌈a_i/x⌉ for each i.

## Solution

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    total_sum = sum(a)
    
    # Maximum beauty: no merging
    max_beauty = sum((val + x - 1) // x for val in a)
    
    # Minimum beauty: merge all elements into one
    min_beauty = (total_sum + x - 1) // x
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)
```

For example, if $x = 3$, and the array is $[4, 11, 6]$, the beauty of the array is equal to $\left\lceil \frac{4}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{11}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{6}{3} \right\rceil = 2 + 4 + 2 = 8$.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array length)
3. Beauty is defined as sum of ceil(element/x) for all elements
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want each element to be as "independent" as possible
- The maximum beauty occurs when we don't combine any elements (keep original array)
- This is because ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x) in general
- So maximum beauty = sum of ceil(a_i/x) for all i

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to combine elements strategically
- The key insight: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)
- This means combining elements reduces the total beauty
- We want to combine elements to minimize the sum
- The minimum beauty is ceil(total_sum/x)

Wait, let me verify this with the examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Original: ceil(3/3) + ceil(6/3) + ceil(9/3) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- Total sum: 18, ceil(18/3) = 6
- Both give 6, so minimum = maximum = 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3
- Original: ceil(6/3) + ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- Total sum: 21, ceil(21/3) = 7
- Can we achieve 7? Yes by combining 4 and 11 to get 15: [6,15]
- ceil(6/3) + ceil(15/3) = 2 + 5 = 7 ✓

The key property: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)

This means combining elements never increases beauty, and sometimes decreases it.

Therefore:
- **Maximum beauty** = sum of ceil(a_i/x) for all i
- **Minimum beauty** = ceil(sum of all a_i / x)

## Algorithm

1. Calculate maximum beauty by summing ceil(a_i/x) for each element
2. Calculate minimum beauty by taking ceil(total_sum / x)

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceil(a_i/x)
    max_beauty = 0
    for num in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceil(total_sum / x)
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_val, max_val = solve()
    print(min_val, max_val)
```

## Verification

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- max_beauty: ceil(3/3) + ceil(6/3) + ceil(9/3) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- min_beauty: ceil(18/3) = 6
- Output: 6 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3  
- max_beauty: ceil(6/3) + ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- min_beauty: ceil(21/3) = 7
- Output: 7 8 ✓

The solution works! The key insight is that combining adjacent elements can only reduce or maintain beauty, never increase it, so the minimum beauty is achieved when all elements are combined into one, giving ceil(total_sum/x).

        max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)

    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given an array $a$ of length $n$, and an integer $x$. You can perform the following operation as many times as you would like (possibly zero): replace two adjacent elements of the array by their sum. For example, if the initial array was $[3, 6, 9]$, in a single operation one can replace the last two elements by their sum, yielding an array $[3, 15]$, or replace the first two elements to get an array $[9, 9]$. Note that the size of the array decreases after each operation.

The beauty of an array $b=[b_1, \ldots, b_k]$ is defined as $\sum_{i=1}^k \left\lceil \frac{b_i}{x} \right\rceil$, which means that we divide each element by $x$, round it up to the nearest integer, and sum up the resulting values. For example, if $x = 3$, and the array is $[4, 11, 6]$, the beauty of the array is equal to $\left\lceil \frac{4}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{11}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{6}{3} \right\rceil = 2 + 4 + 2 = 8$.

Please determine the minimum and the maximum beauty you can get by performing some operations on the original array.


-----Input-----

The first input line contains a single integer $t$ — the number of test cases ($1 \le t \le 1000$).

The first line of each test case contains two integers $n$ and $x$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$, $1 \leq x \leq 10^9$).

The next line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \leq a_i \leq 10^9$), the elements of the array $a$.

It is guaranteed that the sum of values of $n$ over all test cases does not exceed $10^5$.


-----Output-----

For each test case output two integers — the minimal and the maximal possible beauty.


-----Examples-----

Input
2
3 3
3 6 9
3 3
6 4 11
Output
6 6
7 8


-----Note-----

In the first test case the beauty of the array does not change if we perform any operations.

In the second example we can leave the array unchanged to attain the maximum beauty, and to get the minimum beauty one can replace two elements $4$ and $11$ with their sum, yielding an array $[6, 15]$, which has its beauty equal to $7$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace any two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size)
3. Beauty = sum of ceil(element/x) for all elements
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want as many "ceil operations" as possible
- Since ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x), we generally want to keep elements separate
- The maximum beauty is achieved when we don't combine any elements at all
- So maximum beauty = sum of ceil(a[i]/x) for all i

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to combine elements optimally
- When we combine two elements a and b into (a+b), we get ceil((a+b)/x)
- We need to find if ceil((a+b)/x) < ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) (which it usually is)
- The minimum beauty is achieved when we combine elements optimally to reduce the sum
- However, there's a key insight: we can only reduce the total sum by combining elements when the ceiling function "rounds down" for the sum

Actually, let me think more carefully:

For any two numbers a and b:
ceil((a+b)/x) ≤ ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x)

The equality holds when (a+b) % x < x, and the inequality can be strict.

But we want to minimize the total sum, so we should combine elements when ceil((a+b)/x) < ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x).

However, there's a crucial observation: we can combine elements in any way, and the minimum beauty is actually related to the total sum divided by x.

Wait, let me reconsider:

The key insight is that:
- Maximum beauty = sum of ceil(a[i]/x) for all i
- Minimum beauty = ceil(total_sum / x)

Why is this?

Because when we combine elements, we're essentially replacing multiple elements with one larger element. The ceiling function has the property that:
ceil((a+b)/x) ≤ ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x)

And the sum of all ceil operations can be bounded below by ceil(total_sum / x) because:
If we have elements summing to S, after any combination, the sum of the new elements is still S, and beauty = sum of ceil(new_element/x).

Since ceil(S/x) ≤ sum of ceil(element/x) for any grouping, and we can achieve equality when we put everything in one element, the minimum beauty is at least ceil(total_sum / x).

Can we achieve ceil(total_sum / x)? Yes - by combining all elements into one, we get ceil(total_sum / x).

So:
- Maximum beauty = sum(ceil(a[i]/x))
- Minimum beauty = ceil(sum(a)/x)

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Total sum = 18, ceil(18/3) = 6
- Each ceil: ceil(3/3)=1, ceil(6/3)=2, ceil(9/3)=3, sum = 6
- Result: 6 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3  
- Total sum = 21, ceil(21/3) = 7
- Sum of ceilings: ceil(6/3)=2, ceil(4/3)=2, ceil(11/3)=4, sum = 8
- Result: 7 8 ✓

Perfect! This matches the expected output.

## Solution

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceil(a[i]/x) for all i
    max_beauty = 0
    for num in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceil(total_sum / x)
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size)
3. Beauty = sum of ceil(element/x) for all elements
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to keep as many individual elements as possible
- The maximum beauty occurs when we don't perform any operations (keep the original array)
- This is because ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≤ ceil((a+b)/x) in some cases, but we need to check when it's larger
- Actually, we need to think about when combining elements increases or decreases beauty

Wait, let me reconsider. The ceiling function has a special property:
ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)

This is a known mathematical property. So when we combine two elements, the beauty can only decrease or stay the same.

Therefore, **maximum beauty** = sum of ceil(a_i/x) for all original elements.

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to combine elements strategically
- The key insight is that we can always combine elements to reduce the total beauty
- The minimum beauty occurs when we combine all elements into one (the sum of all elements)
- Beauty in this case = ceil(sum(a)/x)

But is this always the minimum? Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Original beauty: ceil(3/3)+ceil(6/3)+ceil(9/3) = 1+2+3 = 6
- Combined beauty: ceil((3+6+9)/3) = ceil(18/3) = 6
- Answer: 6 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3
- Original beauty: ceil(6/3)+ceil(4/3)+ceil(11/3) = 2+2+4 = 8
- Combined beauty: ceil((6+4+11)/3) = ceil(21/3) = 7
- Answer: 7 8 ✓

This matches the expected output!

## Why this works

1. **Maximum beauty**: The original array gives us the maximum because combining elements can only reduce or maintain beauty (due to the property of ceiling function: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x))

2. **Minimum beauty**: Combining all elements into one gives us the minimum because we have no choice but to reduce the total number of terms, and each combination reduces the total beauty due to the ceiling property.

The mathematical property is: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)
This means combining two elements can never increase the total beauty.

Therefore, the minimum beauty is achieved when we combine everything into one element: ceil(sum(a)/x)

## Solution

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceil(a_i / x) for each element
    max_beauty = 0
    for num in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceil(sum(a) / x)
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace any two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size)
3. Beauty = sum of ceil(element/x) for all elements
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to minimize the number of merges
- The maximum beauty is achieved when we don't merge anything - the original array
- This is because ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) >= ceil((a+b)/x) due to the ceiling function property
- So maximum beauty = sum of ceil(a_i/x) for all i

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to merge adjacent elements strategically
- When we merge two elements a and b into (a+b), the beauty changes from ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) to ceil((a+b)/x)
- Since ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x), merging always decreases or maintains beauty
- The minimum beauty will be achieved when we merge optimally to minimize the sum
- The key insight: we need to find the minimum possible value of sum of ceil(sums/x) where sums are partitioning of the original array elements

Wait, let me think more carefully about the minimum beauty.

Actually, when we merge elements, the sum of the array remains constant. The beauty is the sum of ceil(element/x) for each resulting element.

The crucial property: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x), with equality when a/x and b/x don't have fractional parts that add up to cross a boundary.

This means merging always reduces or maintains beauty, so the minimum beauty should be achievable by merging optimally to minimize the total ceiling sum.

But is there a specific pattern? Let me consider:

- If we merge all elements into one, the beauty is ceil(total_sum/x)
- Is this always the minimum? Yes, because merging reduces the beauty.

But wait - is ceil(total_sum/x) always the minimum? Yes, because any partition will have beauty ≥ ceil(total_sum/x) due to the property that ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x).

Actually, I need to verify this property:
- If a/x = k + f where f < 1, then ceil(a/x) = k+1
- Similarly for b
- The sum of ceilings is at least (k1 + k2) + 1 for the fractional parts
- The ceiling of (a+b)/x might be less than or equal to this sum

The key insight: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x) - this is always true!

Therefore, merging elements never increases beauty, and sometimes decreases it. So the minimum beauty is achieved when we merge everything into one element: ceil(sum(a)/x).

Wait, let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Original beauty: ceil(3/3) + ceil(6/3) + ceil(9/3) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- Total sum: 18, ceil(18/3) = 6
- Result: 6, 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3
- Original beauty: ceil(6/3) + ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- Maximum beauty = 8
- Total sum: 21, ceil(21/3) = 7
- Minimum beauty = 7 ✓

This matches the expected output!

## Solution

- Maximum beauty = sum of ceil(a_i / x) for all i
- Minimum beauty = ceil(total_sum / x)

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: don't merge anything
    max_beauty = 0
    for num in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: merge everything into one element
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)
```
