I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size by 1 each time)
3. Beauty is defined as sum of ceiling of each element divided by x
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to keep elements separate because ceiling function tends to increase when we have smaller values
- The maximum beauty is achieved when we don't perform any operations (original array)
- This is because merging adjacent elements might decrease the total ceiling sum due to how ceiling function works

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to merge elements strategically
- The key insight is that we can merge elements to reduce the total ceiling sum
- When we merge two elements a and b into (a+b), the ceiling of their sum might be less than the sum of their individual ceilings

Let me think about the mathematical property:
- For any two numbers a and b: ⌈(a+b)/x⌉ ≤ ⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉
- This is not always true! Actually, ⌈(a+b)/x⌉ ≤ ⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ is generally true, but equality can hold or not.

Wait, let me reconsider. The key insight is:

- The minimum beauty is actually achieved when we merge all elements into a single element
- This is because merging reduces the number of terms in the sum, and we can strategically merge to minimize the total ceiling sum

However, let me think more carefully about when ceiling(A+B)/x ≤ ceiling(A/x) + ceiling(B/x):

Actually, it's the opposite. ⌈(a+b)/x⌉ ≤ ⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ is true, but we want to minimize the total sum.

For minimum beauty, we can merge optimally. The minimum value should be ⌈total_sum/x⌉ because if we merge everything, we get one element whose ceiling is ⌈(sum)/x⌉.

For maximum beauty, we keep all elements as they are, so it's sum of ⌈a_i/x⌉ for all i.

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Original beauty: ⌈3/3⌉ + ⌈6/3⌉ + ⌈9/3⌉ = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- If we merge all: ⌈(3+6+9)/3⌉ = ⌈18/3⌉ = 6
- So both min and max are 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3
- Original beauty: ⌈6/3⌉ + ⌈4/3⌉ + ⌈11/3⌉ = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- If we merge all: ⌈(6+4+11)/3⌉ = ⌈21/3⌉ = 7
- So min is 7, max is 8 ✓

This matches the expected output!

## Solution

- Maximum beauty = sum of ⌈a_i/x⌉ for all i
- Minimum beauty = ⌈(sum of all a_i)/x⌉

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: don't merge anything
    max_beauty = 0
    for num in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: merge everything into one
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_val, max_val = solve()
    print(min_val, max_val)
```

    return min_beauty, max_beauty

for _ in range(t):
    min_val, max_val = solve()
    print(min_val, max_val)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given an array $a$ of length $n$, and an integer $x$. You can perform the following operation as many times as you would like (possibly zero): replace two adjacent elements of the array by their sum. For example, if the initial array was $[3, 6, 9]$, in a single operation one can replace the last two elements by their sum, yielding an array $[3, 15]$, or replace the first two elements to get an array $[9, 9]$. Note that the size of the array decreases after each operation.

The beauty of an array $b=[b_1, \ldots, b_k]$ is defined as $\sum_{i=1}^k \left\lceil \frac{b_i}{x} \right\rceil$, which means that we divide each element by $x$, round it up to the nearest integer, and sum up the resulting values. For example, if $x = 3$, and the array is $[4, 11, 6]$, the beauty of the array is equal to $\left\lceil \frac{4}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{11}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{6}{3} \right\rceil = 2 + 4 + 2 = 8$.

Please determine the minimum and the maximum beauty you can get by performing some operations on the original array.


-----Input-----

The first input line contains a single integer $t$ — the number of test cases ($1 \le t \le 1000$).

The first line of each test case contains two integers $n$ and $x$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$, $1 \leq x \leq 10^9$).

The next line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \leq a_i \leq 10^9$), the elements of the array $a$.

It is guaranteed that the sum of values of $n$ over all test cases does not exceed $10^5$.


-----Output-----

For each test case output two integers — the minimal and the maximal possible beauty.


-----Examples-----

Input
2
3 3
3 6 9
3 3
6 4 11
Output
6 6
7 8


-----Note-----

In the first test case the beauty of the array does not change if we perform any operations.

In the second example we can leave the array unchanged to attain the maximum beauty, and to get the minimum beauty one can replace two elements $4$ and $11$ with their sum, yielding an array $[6, 15]$, which has its beauty equal to $7$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size)
3. Beauty is defined as the sum of ceiling of each element divided by x
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to keep elements as separate as possible
- This means we shouldn't merge any adjacent elements
- So the maximum beauty is simply the sum of ceiling(a_i/x) for all elements

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to merge elements strategically
- The key insight is that ceiling(a/x) + ceiling(b/x) ≥ ceiling((a+b)/x)
- This is because ceiling(a/x) + ceiling(b/x) is at least the ceiling of the sum when we merge
- However, there's an important exception: if a/x and b/x have fractional parts that sum to at least 1, then ceiling(a/x) + ceiling(b/x) = ceiling((a+b)/x)
- Actually, we can always achieve the minimum by merging all elements together
- The minimum beauty should be ceiling(total_sum/x)

Wait, let me reconsider. Is merging all elements together always optimal?

Actually, the relationship between ceiling functions and sum is:
- ceiling(a/x) + ceiling(b/x) ≥ ceiling((a+b)/x)
- The difference is exactly when the fractional parts sum to more than 1

But for minimum beauty, we want to minimize the sum. The optimal strategy is to merge elements in a way that minimizes the total ceiling.

However, it's a known mathematical fact that the sum of ceilings is minimized when we merge all elements together. This is because:
- ceiling(a/x) + ceiling(b/x) ≥ ceiling((a+b)/x)
- Equality holds when the fractional parts don't sum to more than 1

But we can always achieve ceiling(total_sum/x) by merging all elements.

Therefore:
- **Minimum beauty** = ceiling(total_sum / x)
- **Maximum beauty** = sum of ceiling(a_i / x)

Let me verify with the examples:

**Example 1**: [3,6,9], x=3
- Total sum = 18, ceiling(18/3) = ceiling(6) = 6
- Max beauty = ceiling(3/3) + ceiling(6/3) + ceiling(9/3) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- Both are 6 ✓

**Example 2**: [6,4,11], x=3  
- Total sum = 21, ceiling(21/3) = ceiling(7) = 7
- Max beauty = ceiling(6/3) + ceiling(4/3) + ceiling(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- So min=7, max=8 ✓

Perfect! The solution is:

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    total_sum = sum(a)
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceiling of each element divided by x
    max_beauty = 0
    for num in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceiling of total sum divided by x
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given an array $a$ of length $n$, and an integer $x$. You can perform the following operation as many times as you would like (possibly zero): replace two adjacent elements of the array by their sum. Note that the size of the array decreases after each operation.

The beauty of an array $b=[b_1, \ldots, b_k]$ is defined as $\sum_{i=1}^k \left\lceil \frac{b_i}{x} \right\rceil$, which means that we divide each element by $x$, round it up to the nearest integer, and sum up the resulting values. For example, if $x = 3$, and the array is $[4, 11, 6]$, the beauty of the array is equal to $\left\lceil \frac{4}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{11}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{6}{3} \right\rceil = 2 + 4 + 2 = 8$.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace any two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size)
3. Beauty of array = sum of ceil(element/x) for all elements
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want as many "ceil operations" as possible
- Since ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x), we can never increase the total beauty by merging elements
- Actually, ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x) is true, which means merging reduces or maintains beauty
- Therefore, the maximum beauty is achieved when we don't merge anything at all
- Maximum beauty = sum of ceil(a[i]/x) for all i

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to merge elements optimally
- The key insight is that we can always merge elements to get a single element: sum of all elements
- Beauty of the final array with one element = ceil(sum_of_all_elements / x)
- But is this always the minimum?

Wait, let me reconsider. The constraint is that we can only merge adjacent elements. However, by merging adjacent elements repeatedly, we can eventually make the entire array into one element.

The question is: can we always achieve beauty = ceil(total_sum / x)?

Let me think about the mathematical property:
- ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)
- This means merging reduces the total beauty
- So the minimum beauty should be ceil(total_sum / x)

Therefore:
- Maximum beauty = sum of ceil(a[i]/x) for all i
- Minimum beauty = ceil(total_sum / x)

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Max: ceil(3/3) + ceil(6/3) + ceil(9/3) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- Min: ceil((3+6+9)/3) = ceil(18/3) = 6
- Answer: 6 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3
- Max: ceil(6/3) + ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- Min: ceil((6+4+11)/3) = ceil(21/3) = 7
- Answer: 7 8 ✓

Perfect! The solution is:

## Solution

```python
import math

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceil(a[i]/x) for all i
    max_beauty = 0
    for val in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(val / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceil(total_sum / x)
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    print(min_beauty, max_beauty)
```

The algorithm works because:
1. Maximum beauty is achieved when no merges occur (we can't increase beauty by merging)
2. Minimum beauty is achieved when we merge everything into one element, giving us ceil(total_sum / x)
3. Since ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x), merging always decreases or maintains the total beauty
4. Therefore, the minimum possible beauty is ceil(total_sum / x)

Time complexity: O(n) per test case
Space complexity: O(1) extra space (not counting input)
